defined uniquely up to a constant. Note that the Boyer–Lindquist coordinates are only well defined on
\mathcal{M}_{M,a}\setminus\{r=r_{+}\}
.

It will be convenient to state moreover the inverse metric
g^{-1}_{M,a}
:

(6.2)

\begin{split}g^{-1}_{M,a}=&\>a^{2}\rho^{-2}\sin^{2}\theta\partial_{v}\otimes\partial_{v}+\rho^{-2}(r^{2}+a^{2})[\partial_{v}\otimes\partial_{r}+\partial_{r}\otimes\partial_{v}]+\Delta\rho^{-2}\partial_{r}\otimes\partial_{r}\\
&+a\rho^{-2}[(\partial_{v}+\partial_{r})\otimes\partial_{\varphi_{*}}+\partial_{\varphi_{*}}\otimes(\partial_{v}+\partial_{r})]+\rho^{-2}[\partial_{\theta}\otimes\partial_{\theta}+\sin^{-2}\theta\partial_{\varphi_{*}}\otimes\partial_{\varphi_{*}}].\end{split}

The
surface gravity
\kappa_{+}
associated to the future event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}=\{r=r_{+}\}
takes the following expression:

\kappa_{+}=\frac{r_{+}-M}{2Mr_{+}}.

We introduce the following vector fields, defined with respect to
(v,r,\theta,\varphi_{*})
coordinates:

\displaystyle Y=

\displaystyle\>\partial_{r},

\displaystyle T=

\displaystyle\>\partial_{v},

\displaystyle\Phi=

\displaystyle\>\partial_{\varphi_{*}}.

We will now introduce the coordinate chart
(\tilde{v},\varrho,\vartheta,\varphi_{*})
which we motivate by first considering the
M=0
case where the Kerr spacetime is locally isometric to the Minkowski spacetime and the coordinates
(v,r,\theta,\varphi_{*})
correspond to a foliation of the spacetime by axisymmetric ellipsoids with eccentricity parameter
a^{2}
.

In the
M=0
case, the transformation from
(v,r,\theta)
to
(\tilde{v},\varrho,\vartheta)
corresponds to passing from ellipsoids with a non-zero eccentricity parameter to round spheres. More precisely,
(r,\theta)
have the following relation to Cartesian coordinates
(\tilde{x},\tilde{y},\tilde{z})
on Minkowski:

\displaystyle\tilde{z}=

\displaystyle\>r\cos\theta,

\displaystyle\tilde{x}^{2}+\tilde{y}^{2}=

\displaystyle\>(r^{2}+a^{2})\sin^{2}\theta.

Let
(\varrho,\vartheta,\varphi)
denote standard spherical coordinates on Minkowski, then we also have that

\displaystyle\tilde{z}=

\displaystyle\>\varrho\cos\vartheta,

\displaystyle\tilde{x}^{2}+\tilde{y}^{2}=

\displaystyle\>\varrho^{2}\sin^{2}\vartheta.

From the above, it is straightforward to obtain the following relations in Minkowski:

(6.3)

\displaystyle\varrho^{2}=

\displaystyle\>r^{2}+a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta,

(6.4)

\displaystyle\sin^{2}\vartheta=

\displaystyle\>\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{r^{2}+a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}\sin^{2}\theta,

(6.5)

\displaystyle r^{2}=

\displaystyle\>\frac{1}{2}(\varrho^{2}-a^{2})\left(1+\sqrt{1+\frac{4a^{2}\varrho^{2}}{(\varrho^{2}-a^{2})^{2}}\cos^{2}\vartheta}\right),

(6.6)

\displaystyle\sin^{2}\theta=

\displaystyle\>\frac{1}{2a^{2}}\left(\varrho^{2}+a^{2}-\sqrt{(\varrho^{2}+a^{2})^{2}-4a^{2}\varrho^{2}\sin^{2}\vartheta}\right)

Consider now the
M\neq 0
case. In this case, we define
\varrho:[r_{+},\infty)\times(0,\pi)\to[r_{+},\infty)
and
\vartheta:[r_{+},\infty)\times(0,\pi)\to(0,\pi)
as smooth functions satisfying:

\displaystyle\varrho(r,\theta)=

\displaystyle\>\sqrt{r^{2}+\check{a}^{2}\sin^{2}\theta},

\displaystyle(\sin\vartheta)(r,\theta)=

\displaystyle\>\sqrt{\frac{r^{2}+\check{a}^{2}}{r^{2}+\check{a}^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}}\sin\theta,

with
\check{a}:[r_{+},\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
a smooth function with
|\check{a}|\leq|a|
, such that
\check{a}(r)=a
for
r\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}R_{\infty}
and
\check{a}(r)=0
for
r\leq\frac{1}{2}R_{\infty}
. Then
r\leq\varrho(r,\theta)\leq r\sqrt{1+a^{2}r^{-2}}\leq\sqrt{2}r
and, for
r\leq\frac{1}{2}R_{\infty}
we have that
\varrho=r
. Hence, in particular, we have that
\check{a}=a
in the region
\{\varrho\leq\frac{1}{2}R_{\infty}\}
and
\check{a}=0
in the region
\{\varrho\geq R_{\infty}\}
.

Note that for
|a|R_{\infty}^{-1}
suitably small,

\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{r}\varrho&\partial_{r}\vartheta,\\
\partial_{\theta}\varrho&\partial_{\theta}\vartheta\end{pmatrix}

is clearly invertible everywhere.

In particular, for
r\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}R_{\infty}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\vartheta=

\displaystyle\>\frac{a^{2}}{r(r^{2}+a^{2})}\sin^{2}\vartheta\cos^{2}\vartheta,

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\varrho=

\displaystyle\>\frac{r}{\varrho}.

Furthermore, it follows that we can write for all
r\geq r_{+}
:

\displaystyle r(\varrho,\vartheta)=

\displaystyle\>\varrho-f_{r}(\varrho,\vartheta),

\displaystyle\theta(\varrho,\vartheta)=

\displaystyle\>\vartheta-f_{\theta}(\varrho,\vartheta),

where
f_{r}
and
f_{\theta}
are smooth everywhere, analytic in
r\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}R_{\infty}
with respect to the variable
\frac{1}{r}
and with:

\displaystyle f_{r}(\varrho,\vartheta)=

\displaystyle\>a^{2}O_{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\varrho}\right),

\displaystyle f_{\theta}(\varrho,\vartheta)=

\displaystyle\>a^{2}O_{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\varrho^{2}}\right).

In particular,

(6.7)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\varrho=

\displaystyle\>\frac{1}{2\varrho}\partial_{r}(\varrho^{2})=\varrho^{-1}(r+\check{a}\partial_{r}\check{a}\sin^{2}\theta)=1+a^{2}\sin^{2}\vartheta O_{\infty}(\varrho^{-2}),

(6.8)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\vartheta=

\displaystyle\>\frac{\partial_{r}(\sin^{2}\vartheta)}{2\sin\vartheta\cos\vartheta}=\frac{\partial_{r}(\check{a}^{2}(r^{2}+\check{a}^{2}\sin^{2}\theta)^{-1})\sin^{2}\theta\cos^{2}\theta}{2\sin\vartheta\cos\vartheta}=a^{2}O_{\infty}(\varrho^{-3})\sin\vartheta\cos\vartheta

We define
\tilde{v}=t+r_{*}(\varrho)
so that
\tilde{v}=v+r_{*}(\varrho)-r_{*}(r)
. Then
\tilde{v}=v
in
r\leq\frac{R_{\infty}}{2}
.

6.2.
Asymptotically hyperboloidal foliations

We will define a time function
\tau
, such that the level sets of
\tau
, denoted
\Sigma_{\tau}
, are appropriate asymptotically hyperboloidal hypersurfaces. Let

\tau=\tilde{v}-\int_{r_{+}}^{\varrho}\mathbbm{h}(\varrho^{\prime})\,d\varrho^{\prime}-v_{0},

where
v_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
\mathbbm{h}:[r_{+},\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
is a smooth, non-negative function, such that

(6.9)

\displaystyle 2-\mathbbm{h}(1-2M\varrho^{-1})=

\displaystyle\>O(\varrho^{-2}),

(6.10)

\displaystyle\rho^{-2}\left[-\mathbbm{h}(2(r^{2}+a^{2})-\mathbbm{h}\Delta)+a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\right]=g^{-1}_{M,a}(d\tau,d\tau)<

\displaystyle\>0.

By construction,
T(\tau)=1
.

Let
x=\frac{1}{\varrho}
and
h:[0,\frac{1}{r_{+}}]_{x}\to\mathbb{R}
, with

M^{2}h(x)=2x^{-2}-\mathbbm{h}(x^{-1})(x^{-2}-2Mx^{-1}).

By assumption on
\mathbbm{h}
,
h(x)=O(1)
.
We will assume that
h
is analytic in an arbitrarily small neighbourhood of
x=0
and that
h(0)\neq 0
.
By
g^{-1}_{M,a}(d\tau,d\tau)<0
, we then necessarily have that
h(0)>0
.

Note that we can obtain
\tau=t
, by defining instead
\mathbbm{h}:(r_{+},\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
by
\mathbbm{h}(r)=\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}
, which cannot be smoothly extended to
r=r_{+}
and does not satisfy (
6.9
).

If
M=0
, then we can simply take
\mathbbm{h}(\varrho)=2
and
\Sigma_{\tau}
are outgoing null hypersurfaces.

For the sake of later convenience, we moreover assume that
1\leq\mathbbm{h}\leq\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}
in the region
\varrho\leq R_{\infty}
, where
R_{\infty}\gg M
will be defined later.

We can extend the manifolds-with-boundary
\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}}=\{\tau^{\prime}\}\times(0,r_{+}^{-1}]_{x}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\vartheta,\varphi_{*}}
as follows:

\widehat{\Sigma}_{\tau^{\prime}}=\{\tau^{\prime}\}\times[0,r_{+}^{-1}]_{x}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\vartheta,\varphi_{*}}.

We denote moreover
\mathcal{I}^{+}=\mathbb{R}_{\tau}\times\{0\}_{x}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\vartheta,\varphi_{*}}
and also
\Sigma:=\Sigma_{0}
and
\widehat{\Sigma}:=\widehat{\Sigma}_{0}
.

The extended manifold
[0,\infty)_{\tau}\times[0,r_{+}^{-1}]_{x}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\vartheta,\varphi_{*}}
can be equipped with an extension of the conformally rescaled metric
r^{-2}g_{M,a}
and forms a conformal compactification of the domain of dependence
D^{+}(\Sigma)
. The subset
\mathcal{I}^{+}
is called future null infinity. See Figure
1
for an illustration of the above geometric notions.

The tuple
(\tau,\varrho,\vartheta,\varphi_{*})
forms a well-defined coordinate chart on the spacetime. With respect to these coordinates, we can express:

\displaystyle T=

\displaystyle\>\partial_{\tau}\quad\textnormal{and we define}

\displaystyle\Theta:=

\displaystyle\>\partial_{\vartheta},

\displaystyle X:=

\displaystyle\>\partial_{\varrho}-\mathbbm{h}\partial_{\tau}.

Alternatively, we can consider the coordinates
(\tau,x,\vartheta,\varphi_{*})
, to obtain the following expression for
X
:

-X=x^{2}\partial_{x}+\frac{2-M^{2}x^{2}h(x)}{1-2Mx}\partial_{\tau}.

By applying (
6.7
) and (
6.8
), we can further express
Y
in terms of
X
and
\Theta
:

Y=(\partial_{r}\varrho)X+(\partial_{r}\vartheta)\Theta=(1+a^{2}\sin^{2}\vartheta O_{\infty}(\varrho^{-2}))X+a^{2}O_{\infty}(\varrho^{-3})\sin\vartheta\cos\vartheta\Theta.

7.
Preliminaries: differential operators

In this section, we introduce the main differential operators that are relevant for the analysis in the paper. We will first express the Kerr wave operator
\square_{g_{M,a}}
with respect to suitable spacetime coordinates, introduced in §
6
, and we will then write down the corresponding rescaled Laplace-transformed operator
L_{s}
, as well as
\mathfrak{L}_{s,\lambda,\kappa}
, which is a modification of
L_{s}
that plays a leading role in the main estimates. In §
18
, we will briefly discuss analogous objects in the context of Kerr–de Sitter spacetimes.

7.1.
Wave operator

We will first express the wave operator
\square_{g_{M,a}}
with respect to different choices of vector fields, adapted to the spacetime coordinates under consideration. With respect to the vector fields
(T,Y,\partial_{\theta},\Phi)
, introduced in §
6
, we have that:

(7.1)

\square_{g_{M,a}}\phi=\rho^{-2}Y(\Delta Y\phi)+2a\rho^{-2}Y\Phi\phi+\rho^{-2}\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2},\theta}\phi+2\rho^{-2}(r^{2}+a^{2})YT\phi+2\rho^{-2}rT\phi\\
+a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\rho^{-2}T^{2}\phi+2a\rho^{-2}T\Phi\phi.

We will now express
\square_{g_{M,a}}
in terms of the vector fields
(T,X,\Theta,\Phi)
, where we recall that in the region
\{\varrho\leq\frac{R_{\infty}}{2}\}
,
X=Y-\mathbbm{h}T
and
\Theta=\partial_{\theta}
. We also introduce the following spherical differential operators:

\displaystyle\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2},\theta}(\cdot)=

\displaystyle\>\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}(\cdot))+\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\theta}\Phi^{2},

\displaystyle\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2},\vartheta}(\cdot)=

\displaystyle\>\frac{1}{\sin\vartheta}\partial_{\vartheta}(\sin\vartheta\partial_{\vartheta}(\cdot))+\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\vartheta}\Phi^{2}.

We can decompose
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
into azimuthal modes:

f=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}f_{m},

with
\Phi f_{m}=imf_{m}
.

We can also decompose
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
into spherical harmonic modes:

f(\vartheta,\varphi_{*})=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}f_{\ell}(\vartheta,\varphi_{*})=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{\ell\geq|m|}f_{m\ell}Y_{\ell m}(\vartheta,\varphi_{*}),

with
\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2},\vartheta}f_{\ell}=-\ell(\ell+1)f_{\ell}
.

We will reserve the notation
\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
for the Laplacian
\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2},\vartheta}
and
\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
for the covariant derivative
\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2},{\vartheta}}
, both associated to spheres of constant
\tau
and
\varrho
.

First, we restrict to the region
\{\varrho\geq R_{\infty}\}
and use the fact that in this region
\check{a}(r)=0
. In the
M=0
case, the operator
\square_{g_{M,a}}
with respect to
(T,X,\partial_{\vartheta},\Phi)
corresponds simply to the wave operator on Minkowski in outgoing Eddington–Finkelstein coordinates
(\tilde{u},\varrho,\vartheta,\varphi_{*})
, with
T=\partial_{\tilde{u}}
,
X=\partial_{\varrho}
and
\Phi=\partial_{\varphi_{*}}
.

We can therefore express in
\{\varrho\geq R_{\infty}\}
:

\rho^{-2}Y(r^{2}Y\phi)+2a\rho^{-2}Y\Phi\phi+\rho^{-2}\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2},\theta}\phi+2\rho^{-2}(r^{2}+a^{2})YT\phi+2rT\phi+a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\rho^{-2}T^{2}\phi+2a\rho^{-2}T\Phi\phi\\
=\square_{g_{0,a}}\phi=\varrho^{-2}X(\varrho^{2}X\phi)+\varrho^{-2}\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2},\vartheta}\phi+2\varrho^{-1}X(\varrho T\phi).

Hence, in
\{\varrho\geq R_{\infty}\}
, we have that

(7.2)

\square_{g_{M,a}}\phi=\varrho^{-2}X(\varrho^{2}X\phi)+\varrho^{-2}\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2},\vartheta}\phi+2\varrho^{-1}X(\varrho T\phi)-2M\varrho^{-2}Y(rY\phi).

We now turn to the full region
\{r\geq r_{+}\}
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Define
\psi:=\varrho\cdot\phi
. With respect to the coordinates
(\tau,x=\varrho^{-1},\vartheta,\varphi_{*})
, we can write:

(7.3)

\varrho^{3}\square_{g_{M,a}}\phi=\partial_{x}(x^{2}\partial_{x}\psi)+2\partial_{x}\partial_{\tau}\psi+\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2},\vartheta}\psi+Mx\cdot\mathcal{B}^{\infty}\psi+\mathcal{K}^{\infty}\psi,

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\infty}f:=

\displaystyle\>b_{xx}\cdot x^{2}\partial_{x}^{2}f+b_{x\tau}\cdot\partial_{x}\partial_{\tau}f+b_{\tau\tau}\partial_{\tau}^{2}f+a^{2}(xb_{x\vartheta}\cdot x^{2}\partial_{x}+xb_{\tau\vartheta}\partial_{\tau})\sin\vartheta\partial_{\vartheta}f

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\>a^{4}x^{4}\cdot b_{\vartheta\vartheta}(\sin\vartheta\partial_{\vartheta})^{2}f+b_{x}\cdot x\partial_{x}f+a^{2}x^{2}b_{\vartheta}\cdot\sin\vartheta\partial_{\vartheta}f+b_{\tau}\partial_{\tau}f+b\cdot f,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{\infty}f:=

\displaystyle\>-2M^{2}h(0)\partial_{\tau}^{2}f,

where the functions
b_{\star\star},b_{\star},b
, with
\star\in\{x,\tau,\vartheta\}
, are smooth and bounded functions of
(\varrho,\vartheta)
, by (
7.2
). We moreover have that
b_{\star\star},b_{\star},b
are analytic in
[0,x_{0}]_{x}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\theta,\varphi}
and there exist constants
C,B_{1},B_{2}>0
, such that

\frac{1}{(n+m)!}(\|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}b\|_{L^{\infty}([0,x_{0}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}+\|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}b_{\star}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,x_{0}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}+\|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{m}\partial_{x}^{n}b_{\star\star}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,x_{0}]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})})\leq CB_{1}^{n}(a^{2}x_{0}^{2}B_{2})^{m}.

In
\{\varrho\leq\frac{R_{\infty}}{2}\}
we can express:

(7.4)

(Mxb_{\tau\tau}-2M^{2}h(0))=-\mathbbm{h}(2(r^{2}+a^{2})-\Delta\mathbbm{h})+a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta,

which is strictly negative by (
6.10
).

Proof.

Note that the form (
7.3
) and the smoothness property of
b_{\star\star},b_{\star},b
hold trivially in
\{\varrho\leq R_{\infty}\}
. Using that in
\{\varrho\leq\frac{R_{\infty}}{2}\}
, we have that
\varrho=r
and
\vartheta=\theta
, so
Y=X+\mathbbm{h}T
and the property (
7.4
) also follows immediately after expanding the
Y
derivatives in (
7.1
) and considering the factor appearing in front of
T^{2}\phi
.

It remains to verify the stated asymptotic properties of
b_{\star\star},b_{\star},b
as
x\to 0
, for which we can restrict to the region
\{\varrho\geq R_{\infty}\}
. We first recall that:

-X=x^{2}\partial_{x}+\frac{2-M^{2}x^{2}h(x)}{1-2Mx}\partial_{\tau},

so

(7.5)

\varrho X(\varrho^{2}X\phi)+\varrho\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2},\vartheta}\phi-2\varrho^{2}X(\varrho T\phi)=\varrho^{2}X^{2}(\varrho\phi)+2\varrho^{2}XT(\varrho\phi)+\varrho\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2},\vartheta}\phi\\
=\left(\partial_{x}+x^{-2}\frac{2-M^{2}x^{2}h(x)}{1-2Mx}\partial_{\tau}\right)\left(x^{2}\partial_{x}\psi+\frac{2-M^{2}x^{2}h(x)}{1-2Mx}\partial_{\tau}\psi\right)-2\left(\partial_{x}+x^{-2}\frac{2-M^{2}x^{2}h(x)}{1-2Mx}\partial_{\tau}\right)\partial_{\tau}\psi\\
+\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2},\vartheta}\psi\\
=\partial_{x}(x^{2}\partial_{x}\psi)+2\partial_{x}\partial_{\tau}\psi+\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2},\vartheta}\psi-2M^{2}\frac{h(x)}{1-2Mx}\partial_{x}\partial_{\tau}\psi\\
+\frac{4Mx^{-1}-M^{2}h}{(1-2Mx)^{2}}(2-M^{2}x^{2}h(x))\partial_{\tau}^{2}\psi+\partial_{x}\left(\frac{2-M^{2}x^{2}h(x)}{1-2Mx}\right)\partial_{\tau}\psi.

Using that:

Y=(1+a^{2}\sin^{2}\vartheta O_{\infty}(\varrho^{-2}))X+a^{2}O_{\infty}(\varrho^{-3})\sin\vartheta\cos\vartheta\partial_{\vartheta},

we moreover obtain:

(7.6)

-2M\varrho Y(rY\phi)\\
=2Mx^{-1}\left[(1+a^{2}\sin^{2}\vartheta O_{\infty}(x^{2}))\left(x^{2}\partial_{x}+\frac{2-M^{2}x^{2}h(x)}{1-2Mx}\partial_{\tau}\right)+a^{2}O_{\infty}(x^{3})\sin\vartheta\cos\vartheta\partial_{\vartheta}\right](x\psi)\\
-2Mx^{-1}r\left((1+a^{2}\sin^{2}\vartheta O_{\infty}(x^{2}))\left(x^{2}\partial_{x}+\frac{2-M^{2}x^{2}h(x)}{1-2Mx}\partial_{\tau}\right)+a^{2}O_{\infty}(x^{3})\sin\vartheta\cos\vartheta\partial_{\vartheta}\right)^{2}(x\psi).

Using that
r=x^{-1}+a^{2}O(x)
, terms involving
\partial_{\tau}^{2}\psi
appearing on the very right-hand side combine to obtain:

-2Mx^{-1}\frac{2+M^{2}O_{\infty}(x^{2})+a^{2}O_{\infty}(x^{2})}{(1-2Mx)^{2}}(2-M^{2}x^{2}h(x))\partial_{\tau}^{2}\psi,

which will cancel to leading-order in
x
with the
\partial_{\tau}^{2}\psi
term on the very right-hand side of (
7.5
), to obtain a total leading-order term:

-2M^{2}h(0)\partial_{\tau}^{2}\psi

when we consider, via (
7.2
),

\varrho^{3}\square_{g_{M,a}}\phi=\varrho X(\varrho^{2}X\phi)+\varrho\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2},\vartheta}\phi-2\varrho^{2}X(\varrho T\phi)-2M\varrho Y(rY\phi)

in the region
\{\varrho\geq R_{\infty}\}
.

Similarly, the leading-order term in the factor multiplying
\partial_{\tau}\psi
in (
7.6
) is:

-4M\partial_{\tau}\psi,

which cancels with the leading-order contribution in the
\partial_{\tau}\psi
term on the very right-hand side of (
7.5
).

For the remaining coefficients, the asymptotics in
x
follow straightforwardly.

The analyticity properties of
b,b_{\star},b_{\star\star}
in
\{r\geq R_{\infty}\}
follow from the analyticity of
r(\varrho,\vartheta)
by (
6.5
) and the
a^{2}x_{0}^{2}
factor in the
L^{\infty}
estimate follows from the factor
\frac{4a^{2}\varrho^{2}}{(\varrho^{2}-a^{2})^{2}}
in front of the
\vartheta
-dependent term in (
6.5
), which plays the role of a scale factor when taking
\vartheta
-derivatives of
r

∎

7.2.
Laplace-transformed operator

We define the differential operator
L_{s}
to be the Laplace transform of the operator

\varrho^{3}\circ\square_{g}\circ\varrho^{-1}

with respect to the time function
\tau
. More explicitly, for
\widehat{\psi}\in C^{\infty}(\widehat{\Sigma})
:

L_{s}\widehat{\psi}=e^{-s\tau}\varrho^{3}\square_{g}(e^{s\tau}\varrho^{-1}\widehat{\psi}).

In view of (
7.3
), we can then write:

(7.7)

L_{s}=\partial_{x}(x^{2}\partial_{x}(\cdot))+2s\partial_{x}(\cdot)+\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2},\vartheta}+Mx\cdot{B}^{\infty}_{s}+K^{\infty}_{s},

with
B_{s}^{\infty}
and
K_{s}^{\infty}
the Laplace transforms of
\mathcal{B}^{\infty}
and
\mathcal{K}^{\infty}
, defined as follows:

\displaystyle B^{\infty}_{s}f:=

\displaystyle\>b_{xx}\cdot x^{2}\partial_{x}^{2}f+sb_{x\tau}\cdot\partial_{x}f+s^{2}b_{\tau\tau}f+a^{2}(xb_{x\vartheta}\cdot x^{2}\partial_{x}+sxb_{\tau\vartheta})\sin\vartheta\partial_{\vartheta}f

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\>a^{4}x^{4}\cdot b_{\vartheta\vartheta}(\sin\vartheta\partial_{\vartheta})^{2}f+b_{x}\cdot x\partial_{x}f+a^{2}x^{2}b_{\vartheta}\cdot\sin\vartheta\partial_{\vartheta}f+sb_{\tau}f+b\cdot f,

\displaystyle K^{\infty}_{s}f:=

\displaystyle\>-2M^{2}h(0)s^{2}f.

7.3.
The Laplace-transformed operator as a perturbation

The main estimates in this paper will not directly involve
L_{s}
. Instead we will treat
L_{s}
as a
perturbation
of a suitable operator that we will denote
\mathfrak{L}_{s,\lambda}
. Here,
\lambda
will be a large parameter that can be interpreted as an angular frequency shift. In §
16
, we will show that
L_{s}
can be split up in the following way:

(7.8)

L_{s}=\mathfrak{L}_{s,\lambda}+\epsilon_{0}\cdot B_{s}+K_{s,\lambda},

with
B_{s,\lambda}:H^{N_{+}+2}_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}\to H^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}
and
K_{s,\lambda}:H^{N_{+}+1}_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}\to H^{N_{+}+1}_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}
bounded operators, with
H^{N_{+}+1}_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}\subset H^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}
Hilbert spaces that will be introduced in §
8
.

We will also show that that the composition
K_{s,\lambda}\circ R
, with
R:H^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,\delta}\to H^{N_{+}+1}_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}
a suitable bounded operator, is a compact operator and that we can arrange
\epsilon_{0}
to be suitably small to obtain
\|\epsilon_{0}B_{s}\circ R\|<1
. This splitting of
L_{s}
will allow us to obtain
meromorphicity
of the map
s\mapsto L_{s}^{-1}
from the invertibility of
\mathfrak{L}_{s,\lambda}
for
s
restricted to suitable domains, via the analytic Fredholm theorem.

We will in fact introduce a family of operators
\mathfrak{L}_{s,\lambda,\kappa}
, where
\kappa\in[0,\infty)
, with
\mathfrak{L}_{s,\lambda}:=\mathfrak{L}_{s,\lambda,0}
. The parameter
\kappa>0
plays a regularizing role, which can be interpreted as making “surface gravity of future-null infinity
\mathcal{I}^{+}
” positive. These operators can also be thought of as simplifications of the operators that are relevant on Kerr–de Sitter spacetimes.

Using that
\varrho=r
and
\vartheta=\theta
in
\{r\leq\frac{1}{2}R_{\infty}\}
and applying (
7.1
), we also have that for
\varrho\leq\frac{1}{2}R_{\infty}
:

r^{-2}\rho^{2}L_{s}=r\partial_{r}(\Delta\partial_{r}(r^{-1}\cdot))+2ar\partial_{r}\partial_{\varphi_{*}}(r^{-1}\cdot)+\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2},\theta}(\cdot)-2r[\mathbbm{h}\Delta-(r^{2}+a^{2})]s\partial_{r}(r^{-1}\cdot)+\left[2r-\frac{d}{dr}(\Delta\mathbbm{h})\right]s(\cdot)\\
+[\mathbbm{h}^{2}\Delta-2\mathbbm{h}(r^{2}+a^{2})+a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta]s^{2}(\cdot)-2a(\mathbbm{h}-1)s\partial_{\varphi_{*}}(\cdot).

Define

r^{-2}\rho^{2}{K}_{s}^{+}f:=\left[2r-\frac{d}{dr}(\Delta\mathbbm{h})\right]s(r^{-1}f)+[\mathbbm{h}^{2}\Delta-2\mathbbm{h}(r^{2}+a^{2})+a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta]s^{2}f-2a(\mathbbm{h}-1)s\partial_{\varphi_{*}}f.

Then we can express in
\{\varrho\leq\frac{1}{2}R_{\infty}\}
:

L_{s}=r^{2}\rho^{-2}\left[r\partial_{r}(\Delta\partial_{r}(r^{-1}\cdot))+2ar\partial_{r}\partial_{\varphi_{*}}(r^{-1}\cdot)+\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}(\cdot)+(4Mr^{2}-2(\mathbbm{h}-1)r\Delta)s\partial_{r}(r^{-1}\cdot)\right]+{K}_{s}^{+}.

Let
\lambda\in[0,\infty)
and define

S_{\lambda}f=2l\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\ell f_{\ell},

with
l\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
the non-negative solution to
l(l+1)=\lambda
. Then, in
\{r\geq R_{\infty}\}
, we can write

(\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}-(\lambda\mathbf{1}+S_{\lambda}))f_{\ell}=-(\ell+l)(\ell+l+1)f_{\ell}.

The operator
-(\lambda\mathbf{1}+S_{\lambda})
therefore has the effect of shifting the angular frequency
\ell
by
l
. Later, we will need to restrict to
\lambda
such that
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
(and we need to take
l
suitably large).

Denote with
\pi_{\leq l}
the projection of
f
to spherical harmonics with angular momentum
\leq l
. Then

(\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}-(\lambda\mathbf{1}+S_{\lambda})\pi_{\leq l})f_{\ell}=-\mathfrak{l}(\mathfrak{l}+1)f_{\ell},

with
\mathfrak{l}=\ell+l
if
\ell\leq l
and
\mathfrak{l}=\ell
if
\ell>l
. We therefore have that
\mathfrak{l}\geq l
for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Let
\chi_{\varrho\geq R_{\infty}}
be a smooth cut-off function that is 1 in
\varrho\geq R_{\infty}
and 0 in
\varrho\leq\frac{1}{2}R_{\infty}
. Let
\chi_{\varrho\leq\frac{1}{2}R_{\infty}}
be a smooth cut-off function that is 1 in
\varrho\leq\frac{1}{2}R_{\infty}
and 0 in
\varrho\geq R_{\infty}

We now define the main differential operator with respect to which we will derive energy estimates and elliptic estimates:

(7.9)

\mathfrak{L}_{s,\lambda,\kappa}:=L_{s}+\kappa\partial_{x}(\chi_{\varrho\geq R_{\infty}}x\partial_{x}(\cdot))-M\chi_{\varrho\geq R_{\infty}}{B}^{\infty}_{s}\\
-(\lambda\pi_{\leq l}+\chi_{\varrho\geq R_{\infty}}S_{\lambda}\pi_{\leq l}+{K}^{\infty}_{s}+\chi_{\varrho\leq\frac{1}{2}R_{\infty}}{K}_{s}^{+}).

In particular,
L_{s}
takes the form (
7.8
) for
\kappa=0
, if we define:

\displaystyle B_{s}:=

\displaystyle\>M\epsilon_{0}^{-1}x\chi_{\varrho\geq R_{\infty}}{B}^{\infty}_{s},

\displaystyle K_{s,\lambda}:=

\displaystyle\>\lambda\pi_{\leq l}+\chi_{\varrho\geq R_{\infty}}S_{\lambda}\pi_{\leq l}+{K}^{\infty}_{s}+\chi_{\varrho\leq\frac{1}{2}R_{\infty}}{K}_{s}^{+}.

Note that
\mathfrak{L}_{s,\lambda}
takes on a rather simple form when
\varrho\in[r_{+},\infty)\setminus(\frac{R_{\infty}}{2},R_{\infty})
:

(7.10)

\displaystyle r^{-2}\rho^{2}\mathfrak{L}_{s,\lambda,\kappa}=

\displaystyle\>r\partial_{r}(\Delta\partial_{r}(r^{-1}\cdot))+2ar\partial_{r}\partial_{\varphi_{*}}(r^{-1}\cdot)+\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}(\cdot)

\displaystyle\>+sr(4Mr-2(1-\mathbbm{h})\Delta)\partial_{r}(r^{-1}\cdot)-\lambda\pi_{\leq l}\quad\textnormal{for}\quad r=\varrho\leq\frac{R_{\infty}}{2},

(7.11)

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{L}_{s,\lambda,\kappa}f)_{\ell}=

\displaystyle\>\partial_{x}(x(\kappa+x)\partial_{x}f_{\ell})+2s\partial_{x}f_{\ell}-\mathfrak{l}(\mathfrak{l}+1)f_{\ell}\quad\textnormal{for}\quad\frac{1}{x}=\varrho\geq R_{\infty}.

In general, we can express:

(7.12)

(\mathfrak{L}_{s,\lambda,\kappa}f)=\partial_{x}(x(x+\kappa\chi_{\varrho\geq R_{\infty}})\partial_{x}f)+2s\partial_{x}f+\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}f-\lambda\pi_{\leq l}f-\chi_{\varrho\geq R_{\infty}}S_{\lambda}\pi_{\leq l}f+\chi_{\varrho<R_{\infty}}({K}_{s}^{\infty}+Mx{B}_{s}^{\infty})\\
-\chi_{\varrho\leq\frac{1}{2}R_{\infty}}{K}_{s}^{+}\quad\textnormal{for all}\quad\varrho\geq r_{+}.

Finally, we define the
\kappa
-regularized operator
L_{s,\kappa}
as follows:

(7.13)

L_{s,\kappa}:=L_{s}+\kappa\partial_{x}(x\chi_{\varrho\geq R_{\infty}})\partial_{x}f).

8.
Preliminaries: Hilbert spaces

In this section, we will define all the Hilbert spaces that are relevant for the present paper.

Let
S\subseteq\Sigma
be closed. We define
H^{k}(S)
as the Hilbert space obtained from the completion of
C^{\infty}(S)
with respect to the Sobolev inner product:

\langle f,g\rangle_{H^{k}(S)}=\sum_{k_{1}+k_{1}\leq k}\int_{S}\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{k_{1}}\partial_{\varrho}^{k_{2}}f\cdot\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{k_{1}}\partial_{\varrho}^{k_{2}}\overline{g}\,\varrho^{2}d\upsigma d\varrho.

Here,
d\upsigma
denotes the following standard volume form on the unit round sphere:
d\upsigma:=\sin\vartheta d\vartheta d\varphi_{*}
.

We denote moreover

\langle f,g\rangle_{H^{k}_{\not{\nabla}}(S)}=\langle f,g\rangle_{H^{k}(S)}+\langle\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}f,\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}g\rangle_{H^{k}(S)},

and we let
H^{k}_{\not{\nabla}}(S)
denote the completion of
C^{\infty}(S)
with respect to this inner product.

Consider
\widehat{\Sigma}\cong[0,r_{+}^{-1}]_{x}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\vartheta,\varphi_{*}}
. Let
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
and
\delta>0
. We introduce the following bilinear forms on
C^{\infty}([0,x_{0}]_{x}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\vartheta,\varphi_{*}})
where
x_{0}>0
,
d_{0}>0
and
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle_{B_{R_{\infty}}}:=

\displaystyle\>\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(d_{0}x_{0})^{2n}}{(\max\{\ell+1,n+1\})^{2n}}\langle\partial_{x}^{n}f_{\ell},\partial_{x}^{n}g_{\ell}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}(x_{0}),

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle_{B_{R_{\infty},2}}:=

\displaystyle\>\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(d_{0}x_{0})^{2n}}{(\max\{\ell+1,n+1\})^{2n}}\Bigg[(\ell+1)^{4}\langle\partial_{x}^{n}f_{\ell},\partial_{x}^{n}g_{\ell}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}(x_{0})

\displaystyle+(\ell+1)^{2}\langle\partial_{x}^{n+1}f_{\ell},\partial_{x}^{n+1}g_{\ell}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}(x_{0})+\langle\partial_{x}^{n+2}f_{\ell},\partial_{x}^{n+2}g_{\ell}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}(x_{0})\Bigg],

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle_{G_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}}:=

\displaystyle\>\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\sum_{n=0}^{\max\{\ell-1,0\}}\frac{(4\sigma)^{2n}(\ell-(n+1))!^{2}}{(\ell+n+1)!^{2}}\int_{0}^{x_{0}}\langle\partial_{x}^{n}f_{\ell},\partial_{x}^{n}g_{\ell}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}(x)\,dx

\displaystyle+(\ell+1)^{3}\sum_{n=\ell}^{\infty}\frac{\sigma^{2n}}{n!^{2}(n+1)!^{2}}\int_{0}^{x_{0}}\langle\partial_{x}^{n}f_{\ell},\partial_{x}^{n}g_{\ell}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}(x)\,dx

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle_{G_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}}:=

\displaystyle\>\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\sum_{n=0}^{\max\{\ell-1,0\}}\frac{(4\sigma)^{2n}(\ell-(n+1))!^{2}}{(\ell+n+1)!^{2}}

\displaystyle\>\times\int_{0}^{x_{0}}(\ell-n)^{2}(\ell+n+1)^{2}\langle\partial_{x}^{n}f_{\ell},\partial_{x}^{n}g_{\ell}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}(x)

\displaystyle+(1+x^{2}(\ell+1)^{2})\langle\partial_{x}^{n+1}f_{\ell},\partial_{x}^{n+1}g_{\ell}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}(x)+x^{4}\langle\partial_{x}^{n+2}f_{\ell},\partial_{x}^{n+2}g_{\ell}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}(x)\,dx

\displaystyle+(\ell+1)^{3}\sum_{n=\ell}^{\infty}\frac{\sigma^{2n}}{n!^{2}(n+1)!^{2}}\int_{0}^{x_{0}}(n+1)^{4}\langle\partial_{x}^{n}f_{\ell},\partial_{x}^{n}g_{\ell}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}(x)

\displaystyle+(1+(n+1)^{2}x^{2})\langle\partial_{x}^{n+1}f_{\ell},\partial_{x}^{n+1}g_{\ell}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}(x)+x^{4}\langle\partial_{x}^{n+2}f_{\ell},\partial_{x}^{n+2}g_{\ell}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}(x)\,dx.

We denote moreover

\displaystyle\|f\|_{B_{R_{\infty}}}:=

\displaystyle\>\sqrt{\langle f,f\rangle_{B_{R_{\infty}}}}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\|f\|_{B_{R_{\infty},2}}:=\sqrt{\langle f,f\rangle_{B_{R_{\infty},2}}}

\displaystyle\|f\|_{G_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}}:=

\displaystyle\>\sqrt{\langle f,f\rangle_{G_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}}}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\|f\|_{G_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}}:=\sqrt{\langle f,f\rangle_{G_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}}}.

Let
w_{p}:[0,r_{+}^{-1}]\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth cut-off function that is defined as follows: let
p\in[0,\infty)
, then

(8.1)

w_{p}(x)=\begin{cases}p&x\leq\frac{1}{4R_{\infty}},\\
0&x\geq\frac{1}{3R_{\infty}}.\end{cases}

Let
N_{+}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
. We fix
p=4(N_{+}+1)+2
. We define the following inner products on
C^{\infty}(\widehat{\Sigma})
: let
x_{0}=\frac{1}{2R_{\infty}}
, then

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle_{H^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}}:=

\displaystyle\>\langle f,g\rangle_{H^{N_{+}}(\Sigma\cap\{\varrho\leq 3R_{\infty}\})}+\langle\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}f,\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}g\rangle_{H^{N_{+}}(\Sigma\cap\{\varrho\leq\frac{1}{3}R_{\infty}\})}

\displaystyle+\langle(\mathbf{1}-\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}})^{-\frac{w_{p}}{4}}f,(\mathbf{1}-\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}})^{-\frac{w_{p}}{4}}g\rangle_{H^{N_{+}}(\Sigma\cap\{3R_{\infty}\leq\varrho\leq 4R_{\infty}\})}+\langle f,g\rangle_{B_{R_{\infty}}}+\langle f,g\rangle_{G_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}},

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle_{H^{N_{+}+1}_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}}:=

\displaystyle\>\sum_{k=0}^{1}\langle\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{k}f,\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{k}g\rangle_{H^{N_{+}+1}(\Sigma\cap\{\varrho\leq\frac{1}{3}R_{\infty}\})}+\langle f,g\rangle_{H^{N_{+}+2}(\Sigma\cap\{\frac{1}{3}R_{\infty}\leq\varrho\leq 3R_{\infty}\})}

\displaystyle+\langle(\mathbf{1}-\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}})^{-\frac{w_{p}}{4}}f,(\mathbf{1}-\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}})^{-\frac{w_{p}}{4}}g\rangle_{H^{N_{+}+2}(\Sigma\cap\{3R_{\infty}\leq\varrho\leq 4R_{\infty}\})}+\langle f,g\rangle_{B_{R_{\infty},2}}+\langle f,g\rangle_{G_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}}.

We define the Hilbert spaces
H^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}
and
H^{N_{+}+1}_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}
as the completions of
C^{\infty}(\widehat{\Sigma})
with respect to the inner products
\langle f,g\rangle_{H^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}}
and
\langle f,g\rangle_{H^{N_{+}+1}_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}}
, respectively, and we denote the corresponding norms by
\|\cdot\|_{H^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{H^{N_{+}+1}_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}}
, respectively.

We also introduce the subspace
H_{\sigma,R_{\infty},1}^{N_{+}+1}\subset H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}
, which is defined as follows:

H_{\sigma,R_{\infty},1}^{N_{+}+1}:=\{f\in H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}\,|\,\partial_{x}f,\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}f\in H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}\},

and we denote
\|f\|_{H_{\sigma,R_{\infty},1}^{N_{+}+1}}^{2}:=\sum_{1\leq k_{1}+k_{2}\leq 1}\|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}^{k_{1}}\partial_{x}^{k_{2}}f\|_{H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}}
.

Let
P:H^{1}(\widehat{\Sigma})\to L^{2}(\widehat{\Sigma})
be the following linear operator:

Pf=\frac{1}{2M^{2}h(0)-b_{\tau\tau}}\left((2+Mxb_{x\tau})\partial_{x}+Ma^{2}\sin\vartheta x^{2}b_{\tau\vartheta}\partial_{\vartheta}+Mxb_{\tau}\mathbf{1}\right).

The role of the operator
P
is explained in Theorem
9.1
. We define the following space:

\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}={H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}(P)\oplus{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}.

where
{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}(P)=\{f\in{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}\,|\,Pf\in{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}\}
and we equip it with the inner product:

\langle(f,g),(\tilde{f},\tilde{g})\rangle_{\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}}=\langle f,\tilde{f}\rangle_{H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}}+\langle Pf,P\tilde{f}\rangle_{H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}}+\langle g,\tilde{g}\rangle_{H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}}

to obtain a Hilbert space of pairs of functions on
\widehat{\Sigma}
. We note the corresponding norm by
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}}
.

9.
Preliminaries: the infinitesimal generator of time translations

We define the time evolution operators
\mathcal{S}(\tau)
associated to the wave equation
\square_{g_{M,a}}\phi=0
and the foliation
\{\widehat{\Sigma}_{\tau}\}_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}}
as follows: let
N\geq 0
and
\tau\in[0,\infty)
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(\tau):H^{N+1}(\widehat{\Sigma})\times H^{N}(\widehat{\Sigma})

\displaystyle\>\to H^{N+1}(\widehat{\Sigma})\times H^{N}(\widehat{\Sigma}),

\displaystyle(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})

\displaystyle\>\mapsto(\psi(\tau,\cdot),\partial_{\tau}\psi(\tau,\cdot)),

where
\psi=\varrho\cdot\phi
, with
\phi:\mathcal{R}\to\mathbb{C}
the solution to
\square_{g_{M,a}}\phi=0
corresponding to initial data
(\psi|_{\Sigma},\partial_{\tau}\psi|_{\Sigma})=(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})
. The map
\mathcal{S}(\tau)
is well-defined, by local-in-time energy estimates on
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\tau}
; see for example
[
AAG23a
]
[Proposition 3.4]. The results in this section also hold with very minimal modifications on Kerr–de Sitter spacetimes for the equation
(\square_{g_{M,a,\Lambda}}-\frac{2}{3}\Lambda)\phi=0
.

From standard, local-in-time energy estimates, it follows moreover that
\{\mathcal{S}(\tau)\}_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}}
form a
C_{0}
(strongly continuous) semigroup, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(0)=

\displaystyle\>{\rm id},

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(\tau_{1})\mathcal{S}(\tau_{2})=

\displaystyle\>\mathcal{S}_{\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}}\quad\textnormal{for all $\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\mathbb{R}$,}

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to 0}\|\mathcal{S}(\tau)(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})-(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})\|_{H^{N+1}(\widehat{\Sigma})\times H^{N}(\widehat{\Sigma})}=

\displaystyle\>0\quad\textnormal{for all $(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})\in H^{N+1}(\widehat{\Sigma})\times H^{N}(\widehat{\Sigma})$}.

We define the
infinitesimal generator
corresponding to the semigroup
\{\mathcal{S}(\tau)\}_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}:

\displaystyle\>C^{\infty}(\widehat{\Sigma})\times C^{\infty}(\widehat{\Sigma})\to H^{N_{+}+1}(\widehat{\Sigma})\times H^{N_{+}}(\widehat{\Sigma}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\>\lim_{\tau\to 0}\frac{1}{\tau}\left(\mathcal{S}(\tau)(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})-(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})\right)\textnormal{for all $(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})\in C^{\infty}(\widehat{\Sigma})\times C^{\infty}(\widehat{\Sigma})$,}

where the limit is taken with respect to the
H^{N_{+}+1}(\widehat{\Sigma})\times H^{N_{+}}(\widehat{\Sigma})
norm.

Note that for
(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})\in C^{\infty}(\widehat{\Sigma})\times C^{\infty}(\widehat{\Sigma})
, the function
\psi=\varrho\phi
with
\phi:D^{+}(\Sigma)\to\mathbb{C}
the solution to
\square_{g_{M,a}}\phi=0
corresponding to initial data
(\psi|_{\Sigma},\partial_{\tau}\psi|_{\Sigma})=(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})
satisfies

\mathcal{A}(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})=(\partial_{\tau}\psi|_{\Sigma},\partial_{\tau}^{2}\psi|_{\Sigma})\in C^{\infty}(\widehat{\Sigma})\times C^{\infty}(\widehat{\Sigma})=:(C^{\infty}(\widehat{\Sigma}))^{2}.

In the lemma below, we will relate
\mathcal{A}
to the operator
L_{s}
introduced in §
7.2
. For this, we introduce the following domain for
L_{s}
:

{\rm dom}\,(L_{s}):=\{f\in C^{\infty}(\widehat{\Sigma})\cap H^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}\,|\,L_{s}f\in H^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}\}.

We define the Hilbert space
D^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}(L_{s})
as the closure of
{\rm dom}\,(L_{s})
with respect to the graph norm
\|\cdot\|_{H^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}}+\|L_{s}(\cdot)\|_{H^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}}
. By construction,
L_{s}
can be extended as a bounded linear operator to obtain:

L_{s}:D^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}(L_{s})\to H^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}.

Proposition 9.1
.

The infinitesimal generator of time translations
\mathcal{A}
(corresponding to Kerr spacetimes) satisfies the following properties:

(i)

Let
s\in\mathbb{C}
. Then we can express:

(9.1)

\mathcal{A}-s\mathbf{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathbf{1}\\
\mathbf{1}&P-s\mathbf{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2M^{2}h(0)-Mxb_{\tau\tau}}L_{s}&0\\
0&\mathbf{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{1}&0\\
-s\mathbf{1}&\mathbf{1}\end{pmatrix}.

(ii)

Assume that
D^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}(L_{s})\subseteq H_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}^{N_{+}+1}
and that the inverse of
L_{s}
,
L_{s}^{-1}:H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}\to D^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}(L_{s})\subseteq H_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}^{N_{+}+1}
, is a bounded linear operator with respect to the operator norm
\|\cdot\|_{H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}\to H_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}^{N_{+}+1}}
. Then the operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(s):\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}\to

\displaystyle\>\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(s)=

\displaystyle\>\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{1}&0\\
s\mathbf{1}&\mathbf{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}L_{s}^{-1}\circ(2M^{2}h(0)-Mxb_{\tau\tau})\mathbf{1}&0\\
0&\mathbf{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}-(P-s\mathbf{1})&\mathbf{1}\\
\mathbf{1}&0\end{pmatrix}

is a bounded linear operator on
\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}
, which satisfies
(\mathcal{A}-s\mathbf{1})\mathcal{R}(s)(f,g)=(f,g)
for all
(f,g)\in\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}\cap(C^{\infty}(\widehat{\Sigma}))^{2}
and
\mathcal{R}(s)(\mathcal{A}-s\mathbf{1})(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})=(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})
for all
(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})\in\textnormal{ran}\,\mathcal{R}(s)\cap(C^{\infty}(\widehat{\Sigma}))^{2}
.

(iii)

Assume that there exists a
s_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
, such that
H^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}(L_{s_{0}})\subseteq H_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}^{N_{+}+1}
and
L_{s_{0}}^{-1}:H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}\to H^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,\delta}(L_{s_{0}})\subseteq H_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}^{N_{+}+1}
is a bounded linear operator with respect to the operator norm

\|\cdot\|_{H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}\to H_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}^{N_{+}+1}}.

Then the operator
\mathcal{A}
can be extended as a closed linear operator on the following domain:

\mathcal{A}:\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}\supseteq\textnormal{ran}\,\mathcal{R}(s_{0})\to\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}

and

D^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}(L_{s})\oplus{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}(P)=\textnormal{ran}\,\mathcal{R}(s)=\textnormal{ran}\,\mathcal{R}(s_{0})

for all
s\in\mathbb{C}
such that
L_{s}^{-1}
is well-defined.

The above results generalize to or Kerr–de Sitter spacetimes with suitably small
M^{2}\Lambda
by replacing the factor
\frac{1}{2M^{2}h(0)-Mxb_{\tau\tau}}
with an analogous factor, depending on the choice of future-horizon-intersecting, spacelike foliation.

Proof.

We will restrict to Kerr in the proof. The Kerr–de Sitter case follows with minimal modifications. We compare the expression for
\varrho^{3}\square_{g_{M,a}}\phi
in (
7.3
) to the expression for
L_{s}
in (
7.7
) in order to obtain:

\varrho^{3}\square_{g_{M,a}}\phi=L_{s}\psi+2\partial_{x}\partial_{\tau}\psi+Mxb_{x\tau}\cdot\partial_{x}\partial_{\tau}\psi+(Mxb_{\tau\tau}-2M^{2}h(0))\partial_{\tau}^{2}\psi+Ma^{2}x^{2}\sin\vartheta b_{\tau\vartheta}\partial_{\tau}\partial_{\vartheta}\psi+Mxb_{\tau}\partial_{\tau}\psi\\
-2s\partial_{x}\psi-sMxb_{x\tau}\cdot\partial_{x}\psi-s^{2}(Mxb_{\tau\tau}-2M^{2}h(0))\psi-Ma^{2}sx^{2}\sin\vartheta b_{\tau\vartheta}\partial_{\vartheta}\psi-Msxb_{\tau}\psi\\
=L_{s}\psi+(Mxb_{\tau\tau}-2M^{2}h(0))\partial_{\tau}^{2}\psi+(2M^{2}h(0)-Mxb_{\tau\tau})P\partial_{\tau}\psi-s(2M^{2}h(0)-Mxb_{\tau\tau})P\psi+s^{2}(2M^{2}h(0)-Mxb_{\tau\tau})\psi.

Using that
\square_{g_{M,a}}\phi=0
, we can therefore express:

\partial_{\tau}^{2}\psi=(2M^{2}h(0)-Mxb_{\tau\tau})^{-1}L_{s}\psi+P\partial_{\tau}\psi-sP\psi+s^{2}\mathbf{1}

and therefore,
\mathcal{A}
takes the following form

\mathcal{A}(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})=\begin{pmatrix}\Psi^{\prime}\\
(2M^{2}h(0)-Mxb_{\tau\tau})^{-1}L_{s}\Psi+(s^{2}\mathbf{1}-sP)\Psi+P\Psi^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}.

The expression for
\mathcal{A}-s\mathbf{1}
in (i) can then be easily verified.

We consider now (ii). We have that

(9.2)

\mathcal{R}(s)(f,g)=\begin{pmatrix}-L_{s}^{-1}\left((2M^{2}h(0)-Mxb_{\tau\tau})((P-s\mathbf{1})f-g)\right),\\
-sL_{s}^{-1}\left((2M^{2}h(0)-Mxb_{\tau\tau})((P-s\mathbf{1})f-g)\right)+f\end{pmatrix}.

We can use that
L_{s}^{-1}:H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}\to D^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}(L_{s})\subseteq H_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}^{N_{+}+1}
is a bounded linear operator and
f,Pf,g\in H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}
for
(f,g)\in\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}
to ensure that
\mathcal{R}(s)
is in fact well-defined and bounded on the domain
\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}
and
\mathcal{R}(s_{0})
.

Note moreover that
(f,g)\in\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}\cap(C^{\infty}(\widehat{\Sigma}))^{2}
implies that
\mathcal{R}(f,g)\in(C^{\infty}(\widehat{\Sigma}))^{2}
. The property
(\mathcal{A}-s\mathbf{1})\mathcal{R}(s)=\mathcal{R}(s)(\mathcal{A}-s\mathbf{1})
can then easily be verified by using the block diagonal form of
\mathcal{A}-s\mathbf{1}
from (i) to formally invert the operator.

We will now prove (iii). First observe that we can extend the domain of
\mathcal{A}
to
\textnormal{ran}\,\mathcal{R}(s_{0})
as follows. Let
(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})\in\textnormal{ran}\,\mathcal{R}(s_{0})
. Then there exists an element
(f,g)\in\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}
such that
(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})=\mathcal{R}(s_{0})(f,g)
. We then define:

(\mathcal{A}-s\mathbf{1})(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime}):=(f,g)

By (ii), this extends the operator
\mathcal{A}
defined on
\textnormal{ran}\,\mathcal{R}(s_{0})\cap(C^{\infty}(\widehat{\Sigma}))^{2}
to
\textnormal{ran}\,\mathcal{R}(s_{0})
. Closedness of
\mathcal{A}-s\mathbf{1}
(and hence
\mathcal{A}
) then follows immediately from the boundedness of its inverse
\mathcal{R}(s_{0})
.

We will now prove the remaining statement in (iii). Let
(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})\in\textnormal{ran}\,\mathcal{R}(s)
. Then there exist
(f,g)\in\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}
satisfying (
9.2
).
From (i), we have that formally

(\mathcal{A}-s_{0})(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})=(\mathcal{A}-s)(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})+(s-s_{0})\begin{pmatrix}\Psi\\
\Psi^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}f+(s-s_{0})\Psi\\
g+(s-s_{0})\Psi^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}.

Motivated by the above, using that
(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})=\mathcal{R}(s)(f,g)
, we define

\displaystyle\tilde{f}:=

\displaystyle\>f+(s-s_{0})\Psi,

\displaystyle\tilde{g}:=

\displaystyle\>g+(s-s_{0})\Psi^{\prime}.

It then follows that
(\tilde{f},\tilde{g})\in\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{R}(s_{0})(\tilde{f},\tilde{g})=(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})
, so
(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})\in\textnormal{ran}\,\mathcal{R}(s_{0})
. We conclude that
\textnormal{ran}\,\mathcal{R}(s)\subseteq\textnormal{ran}\,\mathcal{R}(s_{0})
. Reversing the roles of
s_{0}
and
s
in the above argument allows us to conclude that
\textnormal{ran}\,\mathcal{R}(s)=\textnormal{ran}\,\mathcal{R}(s_{0})
.
∎

The operator
\mathcal{R}(s)
from Proposition
9.1
satisfies the defining properties of the
resolvent operator
corresponding to
\mathcal{A}:\textnormal{ran}\,\mathcal{R}(s_{0})\to\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}
, where
L_{s_{0}}^{-1}
is well-defined. We can denote

(\mathcal{A}-s\mathbf{1})^{-1}:=\mathcal{R}(s).

Corollary 9.2
.

Assume that for
s\in O
, with
O\subset\mathbb{C}
a connected domain,
either

L_{s}^{-1}:H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}\to D^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}(L_{s_{0}})\subseteq H_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}^{N_{+}+1}
is bounded with respect to the operator norm
\|\cdot\|_{H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}\to H_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}^{N_{+}+1}}
,
or

\ker L_{s}\neq\{0\}
. Assume also that there exists a
s_{0}\in O
such that
\ker L_{s_{0}}=\{0\}
.

Consider
\mathcal{A}:\textnormal{ran}\,\mathcal{R}(s_{0})\to\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}
. Then the spectrum
{\rm Spect}\,(\mathcal{A})
satisfies:

{\rm Spect}\,(\mathcal{A})\cap O={\rm Spect}_{\rm point}\,(\mathcal{A})\cap O=\{s\in O\,|\,\ker L_{s}\neq\{0\}\}.

Furthermore,
(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})\in\textnormal{ran}\,\mathcal{R}(s_{0})
is an eigenvector of
\mathcal{A}
if and only if
(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})=(\widehat{\psi}_{s},s\widehat{\psi}_{s})
, with
\widehat{\psi}_{s}\in\ker L_{s}
.

Proof.

In view of the form of
\mathcal{R}(s)
from (ii) of Proposition
9.1
, boundedness of
\mathcal{R}(s)
is equivalent to boundedness of
L_{s}^{-1}
. Hence,
s\in{\rm Spect}\,(\mathcal{A})\cap O
if and only if
L_{s}^{-1}
is not bounded. By assumption, the latter implies that
\ker L_{s}\neq\{0\}
. Let
\widehat{\psi}_{s}\in\ker L_{s}
. Then

\mathcal{A}(\widehat{\psi}_{s},s\widehat{\psi}_{s})=s\cdot(\widehat{\psi}_{s},s\widehat{\psi}_{s})\in\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}.

and
(f,g)=(\mathcal{A}-s_{0}\mathbf{1})(\widehat{\psi}_{s},s\widehat{\psi}_{s})=(s-s_{0})(\widehat{\psi}_{s},s\widehat{\psi}_{s})\in\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}
, so
\mathcal{R}(s_{0})(f,g)=\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{\psi}_{s}\\
s\widehat{\psi}_{s}\end{pmatrix}
and therefore
(\widehat{\psi},s\widehat{\psi}_{s})\in\textnormal{ran}\,\mathcal{R}(s_{0})
, so
s\in{\rm Spect}_{\rm point}\,(\mathcal{A})\cap O
, with
\mathcal{A}
defined on the domain
\textnormal{ran}\,\mathcal{R}(s_{0})
.
∎

The
Hermitian adjoint
of
L_{s}:D^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}(L_{s})\supset{\rm dom}\,(L_{s})\to H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}
, with respect to the
H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}
inner product is defined as follows: let

D_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}(L_{s}^{*}):=\left\{f\in H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}\,,\,\langle f,L_{s}(\cdot)\rangle_{H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}}:D_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}(L_{s})\supset{\rm dom}\,(L_{s})\to\mathbb{C}\>\textnormal{is bounded}\right\}.

By the Riesz representation theorem, we have that for all
f\in D_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}(L_{s}^{*})
, there exists a unique
g\in D^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}(L_{s})
, such that
\langle g,\widehat{\psi}\rangle_{H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}}=\langle f,L_{s}\widehat{\psi}\rangle_{H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}}
for all
\widehat{\psi}\in{\rm dom}\,(L_{s})
. Then we define the Hermitian adjoint of
L_{s}
by
L_{s}^{*}:D_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}(L_{s}^{*})\to H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}
, with
L_{s}^{*}f=g
, with
f,g
as above.

Similarly, we define the Hermitian adjoint of
\mathcal{A}
, with respect to the
\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}
inner product, as follows: let
s_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
be such that
L_{s_{0}}^{-1}
is well-defined, then

\mathbf{D}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}(\mathcal{A}^{*}):=\left\{(f,g)\in\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}\,,\,\langle f,\mathcal{A}(\cdot)\rangle_{\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}}:{\rm ran}\,\mathcal{R}(s_{0})\to\mathbb{C}\>\textnormal{is bounded}\right\}.

By the Riesz representation theorem, we have that for all
(f,g)\in\mathbf{D}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}(\mathcal{A}^{*})
, there exists a unique
(\Phi,\Phi^{\prime})\in{\rm ran}\,\mathcal{R}(s_{0})
, such that
\langle(\Phi,\Phi^{\prime}),(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}}=\langle(f,g),\mathcal{A}(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}}
for all
(\Psi,\Psi^{\prime})\in(C^{\infty}(\hat{\Sigma}))^{2}\cap\mathcal{R}(s_{0})
. Then we define the Hermitian adjoint of
\mathcal{A}
by
\mathcal{A}^{*}:\mathbf{D}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}(\mathcal{A}^{*})\to\mathbf{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}
, with
\mathcal{A}^{*}(f,g)=(\Phi,\Phi^{\prime})
, with
(f,g)
and
(\Phi,\Phi^{\prime})
as above.

Taking the formal adjoint of the right-hand side of (
9.1
), we obtain

\mathcal{A}^{{\dagger}}-\overline{s}\mathbf{1}:=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{1}&-\overline{s}\mathbf{1}\\
0&\mathbf{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2M^{2}h(0)-Mxb_{\tau\tau}}L_{s}^{*}&0\\
0&\mathbf{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathbf{1}\\
\mathbf{1}&P^{*}-\overline{s}\mathbf{1}\end{pmatrix}.

From the above expression, it follows that
\mathcal{A}^{{\dagger}}(f,g)
is well-defined and agrees with
\mathcal{A}^{*}(f,g)
if
g\in D_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}(L_{s}^{*})\cap D_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}(P^{*})
and
f\in H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}
, with
D_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}(P^{*})
the domain of the Hermitian adjoint
P^{*}
, with respect to the inner product on
H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}
.

A direct corollary of the standard orthogonality property between the kernel of a linear operator and the range of its Hermitian adjoint results in the following:

Corollary 9.3
.

Assume additionally that there exists a
s_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
such that
\ker L_{s_{0}}=\{0\}
. We have that

\displaystyle\textnormal{ran}(\mathcal{A}^{*}-\overline{s}\mathbf{1})\perp

\displaystyle\>\ker(\mathcal{A}-s\mathbf{1}),

\displaystyle\ker(\mathcal{A}^{*}-\overline{\tilde{s}}\mathbf{1})\perp

\displaystyle\>\ker(\mathcal{A}-s\mathbf{1})\quad\textnormal{if $s\neq\tilde{s}$}.

Let
\widehat{\psi}_{s}\in\ker L_{s}
and denote
(\Psi_{s},\Psi^{\prime}_{s})=(\widehat{\psi}_{s},s\widehat{\psi}_{s})\in\ker(\mathcal{A}-s\mathbf{1})
. Let
g_{\tilde{s}}\in\ker L_{\tilde{s}}^{*}
and denote
(\tilde{\Psi}_{\tilde{s}},\tilde{\Psi}^{\prime}_{\tilde{s}})=(-(P^{*}-\overline{\tilde{s}})g_{\tilde{s}},g_{\tilde{s}})
. Then
(\tilde{\Psi}_{\tilde{s}},\tilde{\Psi}^{\prime}_{\tilde{s}})\in\ker(\mathcal{A}^{{\dagger}}-\overline{\tilde{s}}\mathbf{1})
and

\langle(\Psi_{s},\Psi^{\prime}_{s}),(\tilde{\Psi}_{\tilde{s}},\tilde{\Psi}^{\prime}_{\tilde{s}})\rangle_{\mathbf{H}^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}}=0

if
s\neq\tilde{s}
.

10.
Precise statements of the main theorems

In this section, we give precise statements of the main theorems of the paper, making use of the preliminaries introduced in §§
6
–
9
, and we give precise definitions of the notions of
regularity quasinormal modes
and
regularity quasinormal frequencies
.

10.1.
Regularity quasinormal frequencies

The following theorem provides a precise formulation of Theorem
1.1
(i)–(ii) and includes also an additional statement on orthogonality properties of regularity quasinormal modes.

Theorem 10.1
.

Let
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
and
N_{+}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Let

\displaystyle\Omega_{\sigma}:=

\displaystyle\>\left\{x+iy\in\mathbb{C}\,|\,x\in\mathbb{R}_{-},\,y\in\mathbb{R},\,x^{2}+y^{2}<\sigma^{2},\,3y^{2}-5x^{2}>\sigma^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\cup\left\{x+iy\in\mathbb{C}\,|\,x\in[0,\infty),\,\frac{\sigma^{2}}{4}<x^{2}+y^{2}<\sigma^{2}\right\},

\displaystyle\Omega_{\sigma,N_{+}}:=

\displaystyle\>\Omega_{\sigma}\cap\left\{\re z>-\left(\frac{1}{2}+N_{+}\right)\kappa_{+}\right\}

(i)

Then there exists a suitably large
R_{\infty}>r_{+}
such that the following restrictions of the infinitesimal generator of time translations corresponding to
\mathcal{S}(\tau)
are closed linear operators:

\mathcal{A}:\mathbf{H}^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}\supset H^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}(L_{s})\oplus{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}(P)\to\mathbf{H}^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}

and

{\rm Spect}\,(\mathcal{A})\cap\Omega_{\sigma,N_{+}}={\rm Spect}_{\rm point}\,(\mathcal{A})\cap\Omega_{\sigma,N_{+}}

consists of isolated eigenvalues, which are independent of the precise choice of
\tau
. We define
regularity quasinormal frequencies
to be the union of eigenvalues:

\mathscr{Q}_{\rm reg}:=\bigcup_{N_{+}\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\sigma\in\mathbb{R}}{\rm Spect}_{\rm point}\,(\mathcal{A})\cap\Omega_{\sigma,N_{+}}

and
regularity quasinormal modes
to be the corresponding eigenvectors. The sets
{\rm Spect}_{\rm point}\,(\mathcal{A})\cap\Omega_{\sigma,N_{+}}
are independent of the precise choice of foliation-defining function
\mathbbm{h}
.

(ii)

The union
\Omega:=\bigcup_{N_{+}\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\sigma\in\mathbb{R}}\Omega_{\sigma,N_{+}}
satisfies

\Omega=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}\,,\,|{\rm arg\,z}|<\frac{2}{3}\pi\right\}.

The set of quasinormal frequencies
\mathscr{Q}_{\rm reg}
has no accumulation points in
\Omega
and is independent of the choice of
R_{\infty}
.

(iii)

The eigenspaces of
\mathcal{A}:\mathbf{H}^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}\supset H^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}(L_{s})\oplus{H}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}(P)\to\mathbf{H}^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}
are finite dimensional.

(iv)

We define
regularity quasinormal co-modes
to be eigenvectors of
\mathcal{A}^{{\dagger}}
in
((D_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}})(L_{s}^{*})\cap D_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}(P^{*}))\oplus H^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}
. The following orthogonality relations hold with respect to the inner product on
\mathbf{H}^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}
:

\displaystyle\textnormal{ran}(\mathcal{A}^{{\dagger}}-\overline{s}\mathbf{1})\perp

\displaystyle\>\ker(\mathcal{A}-s\mathbf{1}),

\displaystyle\ker(\mathcal{A}^{{\dagger}}-\overline{\tilde{s}}\mathbf{1})\perp

\displaystyle\>\ker(\mathcal{A}-s\mathbf{1})\quad\textnormal{if $s\neq\tilde{s}$}.

Proof.

Theorem
10.1
follows directly from Theorem
10.2
below, in combination with Corollaries
9.2
and
9.3
.
∎

Figure 3
.
A (schematic) pictorial representation of the following subsets of
\mathbb{C}
:
\Omega
(the sector of the complex plane that is not dashed),
\Omega_{\sigma}
(the shaded region), and possible regularity quasinormal frequencies represented by crosses.

The proof of Theorem
10.1
uses crucially the meromorphicity of the inverse
L_{s}^{-1}
of the Laplace transform of the wave operator in Kerr and
L_{s,\Lambda}^{-1}
in Kerr–de Sitter (see §
18
for a definition of
L_{s,\Lambda}
). Meromorphicity in this context is defined as follows:

Definition 10.1
.

Let
O\subset\mathbb{C}
be a connected and open subset,
X,Y
Banach spaces and
B(X,Y)
the space of bounded linear operators from
X
to
Y
. Let
A:O\to B(X,Y)
be a family of bounded operators. Then
A
or
\{A(s)\}
is
meromorphic
if for any
s_{0}\in O
there exists a small neighbourhood
U
of
s_{0}
, such that for all
s\in U
, we can write
A(s)=A_{0}(s)+\sum_{k=1}^{m}(s-s_{0})^{-k}A_{k}
in
U
, where
A_{0}(s)
is holomorphic at
s_{0}
, i.e.
\lim_{s\to s_{0}}\frac{A_{0}(s)-A(s_{0})}{s-s_{0}}
is well-defined with respect to the operator topology on
B(X,Y)
. If
m>0
for some
s_{0}\in O
, we refer to
s_{0}
as a
pole
of
A
.

The orthogonality properties of regularity quasinormal modes are related to the cokernel of
L_{s}
, which is defined as follows:

Definition 10.2
.

Let
A:X\to Y
be a linear operator between Banach spaces
X,Y
. Then the
cokernel of
A
is defined as the quotient space
\textnormal{coker}\,A=Y/\textnormal{ran}\,A
.

The following theorem is a precise version of Theorem
4.1
(i)–(ii) and includes an additional statement on the orthogonality relations between the kernel and cokernel of
L_{s}
.
This theorem constitutes the core of the present paper.

Theorem 10.2
.

Let
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
and
N_{+}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

(i)

For suitably large
R_{\infty}>r_{+}
and suitably small
M^{2}\Lambda\geq 0
, the family of operators
\{L_{s,\Lambda}^{-1}:H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}\to H_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}^{N_{+}+1}\}
with
s\in\Omega_{\sigma,N_{+}}
is meromorphic. We denote with
\mathscr{Q}_{\sigma,R_{\infty},N_{+}}
the set of poles of
\{L_{s,\Lambda}^{-1}\}
, which does not depend on the precise choice of foliation-defining function
\mathbbm{h}
. Furthermore, there exists a
C_{0}\geq 0
such that the family of operators is holomorphic when restricted to
s\in\{\re z>C_{0}\}
.

(ii)

Let
\widetilde{R}_{\infty}\geq R_{\infty}
,
\widetilde{N}_{+}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\widetilde{\sigma}\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

\mathscr{Q}_{\sigma,\widetilde{R}_{\infty},\widetilde{N}_{+}}\cap\Omega_{\sigma,N_{+}}\cap\Omega_{\widetilde{\sigma},\widetilde{N}_{+}}=\mathscr{Q}_{\sigma,R_{\infty},N_{+}}\cap\Omega_{\sigma,N_{+}}\cap\Omega_{\widetilde{\sigma},\widetilde{N}_{+}}

and
\bigcup_{N_{+}\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\sigma\in\mathbb{R}}\mathscr{Q}_{\sigma,R_{\infty},N_{+}}
has no accumulation points in
\Omega
.

(iii)

Let
s\in\Omega_{\sigma,N_{+}}
. Then the space
\ker L_{s,\Lambda}
is finite dimensional with
\dim\ker L_{s,\Lambda}=\dim\textnormal{coker}\,L_{s,\Lambda}
and
\textnormal{ran}\,L_{s,\Lambda}^{*}=(\ker L_{s,\Lambda})^{\perp}\subset H_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}^{N_{+}+1}
, with
L_{s,\Lambda}^{*}
the Hermitian adjoint and
\textnormal{coker}\,L_{s,\Lambda}^{*}
isomorphic to
\ker L_{s,\Lambda}
.

Proof.

By standard, local energy estimates for
\square_{g_{M,a}}\phi=0
and a Grönwall inequality, one can obtain uniform constants
C,C_{0}>0
such that:

(10.1)

\|(\phi,\partial_{\tau}\phi)\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma)\times L^{2}(\Sigma)}(\tau)\leq Ce^{C_{0}\tau}\|(\phi,\partial_{\tau}\phi)\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma)\times L^{2}(\Sigma)}(0).

Suppose
\{L_{s,\Lambda}^{-1}\}
had a pole in
s_{0}\in\{\re z>C_{0}\}
. Then the solution
\phi
corresponding to initial data
(\phi_{s_{0}},s\phi_{s_{0}})
with
\psi_{s_{0}}=\varrho\phi_{s_{0}}\in\ker L_{s_{0},\Lambda}
takes the form
(\phi,\partial_{\tau}\phi)(\tau)=(e^{s_{0}\tau}\phi_{s_{0}},s\phi_{s_{0}})
and hence

\|(\phi,\partial_{\tau}\phi)\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma)\times L^{2}(\Sigma)}(\tau)=e^{(\re s_{0})\tau}\|(\phi,\partial_{\tau}\phi)\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma)\times L^{2}(\Sigma)}(0)

for all
\tau\geq 0
, which is in contradiction with (
10.1
). Hence,
\{L_{s,\Lambda}^{-1}\}
is holomorphic when restricted to
\{\re z>C_{0}\}
. In the
\Lambda=0
case, the remaining statement in (i) as well as (iii) follow from Proposition
16.2
below. Part (ii) follows from Proposition
16.4
below. This is then generalized to
\Lambda\geq 0
in Corollary
18.3
.
∎

Remark 10.1
.

Uniform boundedness (and decay) of solutions to the wave equation
\square_{g_{M,a}}\phi=0
, established by Dafermos–Rodnianski–Shlapentokh-Rothman in
[
DRSR16
]
implies that in the
\Lambda=0
case, the set
\mathscr{Q}_{\rm reg}
is in fact entirely contained in the left-half plane
\{\re z<0\}
(
C_{0}
in the proof of Theorem
10.2
(i) can be set to
0
). The validity of Theorem
10.2
, however, is independent of a boundedness statement and applies to settings where such a boundedness statement is false, like the class of wave equations with potentials on Kerr studied in
[
Mos17
]
.

Closely related to uniform boundedness for
\square_{g_{M,a}}\phi=0
, is the statement of “mode stability”
[
Whi89
,
SR15
]
, which implies the absence of regularity quasinormal modes of the form (
10.3
) below. Since regularity quasinormal modes need not satisfy (
10.3
), mode stability alone is not enough to ensure that
\mathscr{Q}_{\rm reg}
is contained in the left-half plane when
\Lambda=0
; see Remark
10.2
below.

For
\Lambda>0
with
M^{2}\Lambda\ll 1
, the results of
[
Hin24
]
imply that
\mathscr{Q}_{\rm reg}
is contained in
\{\re z<0\}
. Note that in the general
\Lambda>0
case however, not even mode stability is known; see
[
CT22
]
for partial results in this direction.

Remark 10.2
.

Consider the following angular ODE:

(10.2)

-\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}S_{m\ell})+\left[\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\theta}m^{2}+a^{2}\omega^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\right]S_{m\ell}=\Lambda_{m\ell}(a\omega)S_{m\ell},

with
S_{m\ell}(\theta;a\omega)
in
L^{2}(\sin\theta d\theta)
the
oblate spheroidal harmonics
. When
a=0
, (
10.2
) is simply the Laplace equation on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
restricted to functions with azimuthal angular dependence
e^{im\varphi}
and the solutions are spherical harmonics.

When
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
, (
10.2
) admits solutions for a discrete set of
\Lambda_{m\ell}(a\omega)\in\mathbb{R}
, labelled by
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq|m|}
such that
\Lambda_{m\ell}(a\omega)\geq\Lambda_{m\ell^{\prime}}(a\omega)
if
\ell\geq\ell^{\prime}
. Furthermore, when
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
, the functions
\{S_{m\ell}(\theta;a\omega)\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\mathbb{Z},l\geq|m|\end{subarray}}
form an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\sin\theta d\theta)
, the self-adjointness of the differential operator on the left-hand side of (
10.2
) and the compactness of its inverse.

The functions
\omega\mapsto\Lambda_{m\ell}(a\omega)
can be analytically continued to the rest of the complex plane as complex functions, with a finite number of branch points, for which the corresponding (
10.2
) admits solutions in
L^{2}(\sin\theta d\theta)
; see for example
[
MS54
]
[Proposition 5 of Chapter 1.5, Proposition 2 of Chapter 3.22] for a proof in the context of general ODE and
[
TdC20
]
[Proposition 2.1] for a discussion on this matter in the context of (
10.2
). However, when
\omega\notin\mathbb{R}
,
\{S_{m\ell}(\theta;a\omega)\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\mathbb{Z},l\geq|m|\end{subarray}}
no longer need to form an orthonormal basis.

In much of the physics literature, see for example
[
Lea85
]
, quasinormal mode solutions
\phi
to the wave equation (
1.1
) are required to take the separated form

(10.3)

\phi(t,r_{*},\theta,\varphi)=e^{-i\omega t}\frac{u_{\ell m}(r_{*};\omega)}{\sqrt{r^{2}+a^{2}}}S_{m\ell}(\theta;a\omega)e^{im\varphi},

with
S_{m\ell}(\theta;a\omega)
a solution to (
10.2
) and
u_{\ell m}(r;\omega)
a solution to the ODE:

(10.4)

u_{\ell m}^{\prime\prime}+(\omega^{2}-V_{m\ell}^{(a\omega)})u_{\ell m}=0,

where
V_{m\ell}^{(a\omega)}(r_{*})
is a potential function that depends on
\Lambda_{m\ell}(a\omega)
.

In the present paper, we do
not
impose the restriction (
10.3
) and we instead allow for more general solutions of the form:

\phi(t,r,\theta,\varphi)=e^{-i\omega t}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\hat{\phi}_{m}(r,\theta)e^{im\varphi}.

In view of the finite dimensionality of the space of regularity quasinormal modes that follows from Theorem
10.1
(iii), we can infer that quasinormal modes corresponding to a single quasinormal frequency
s=-i\omega
must be supported on a finite number of
m
. If
\{S_{m\ell}(\theta;a\omega)\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\mathbb{Z},l\geq|m|\end{subarray}}
formed an orthonormal basis for
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
, then we would also be able to conclude that any regularity quasinormal mode can be written as a finite linear combination of regularity quasinormal modes satisfying (
10.3
). This is the case when
a=0
.

As mentioned above, we are not, however, guaranteed an orthonormal basis of functions
S_{m\ell}
when
a\neq 0
, so we cannot draw such a conclusion. The set of regularity quasinormal frequencies could therefore in principle be
strictly larger
than the set of frequencies corresponding to quasinormal modes that can be separated as in (
10.3
).

10.2.
Application 1: stability of the quasinormal spectrum and convergence of Kerr–de Sitter quasinormal frequencies

We first give a definition of
stability
of the spectrum of a family of linear operators that is suitable for the setting of the present paper.

Definition 10.3

(Stability of the spectrum for a family of restricted operators)
.

Let
O=\bigcup_{\alpha\in I}O_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathbb{C}
, with
O_{\alpha}\subset\mathbb{C}
open and
I
an index set. Let
\mathbf{H}
be a Hilbert space and let
A:{\rm dom}(A)\subseteq\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{H}
be a densely defined linear operator. Let
\mathbf{H}^{\prime}_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathbf{H}_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathbf{H}
be families of closed subspaces, such that the following restrictions of
A
are densely defined operators:
A:\mathbf{H}^{\prime}_{\alpha}\supseteq{\rm dom}(A_{\alpha})\to\mathbf{H}_{\alpha}
. Denote the corresponding restricted operators
A_{\alpha}
.

Let
B
be a linear operator, such that its restrictions to
\mathbf{H}^{\prime}_{\alpha}
results in operators
B_{\alpha}:\mathbf{H}^{\prime}_{\alpha}\to\mathbf{H}_{\alpha}
that are bounded with respect to the operator norms
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbf{H}_{\alpha}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{H}_{\alpha}}
for all
\alpha\in I
.

Then we say that
A
has a
stable spectrum in
O
in the direction
B
, with respect to the the families of subspaces
\{\mathbf{H}_{\alpha},\mathbf{H}^{\prime}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in I}
, if the following holds: for any compact domain
K\subset O
and for all
\epsilon>0
, there exists a
\delta_{0}>0
such that for all
\alpha\in I
and for all
0<\delta\leq\delta_{0}
:

(1)

{\rm Spect}(A_{\alpha}+\delta\cdot B_{\alpha})\cap O_{\alpha}\cap K\subseteq\bigcup_{s\in{\rm Spect}(A_{\alpha})\cap O_{\alpha}}D_{\epsilon}(s)
, with
D_{\epsilon}(s)
the open disc of radius
\epsilon
around
s\in\mathbb{C}
.

(2)

For all
s\in{\rm Spect}(A_{\alpha})\cap O_{\alpha}\cap K
,

{\rm Spect}(A_{\alpha}+\delta\cdot B_{\alpha})\cap K\cap D_{\epsilon}(s)\neq\emptyset.

Figure 4
.
A pictorial representation of stability of the spectrum in the case of a spectrum of isolated eigenvalues of
A_{\alpha}
represented by crosses. The eigenvalues of
A_{\alpha}+\delta\cdot B_{\alpha}
are represented by squares and the shaded disks represent
D_{\epsilon}(s)
with
s\in{\rm Spect}(A_{\alpha})
.

Using the above definition, the following theorem gives a precise formulation of Theorem
1.2
(i):

Theorem 10.3
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{\delta\cdot Q}
denote the infinitesimal generator of time translations corresponding to the following perturbed wave operator:

\square_{g_{M,a}}+\delta\cdot\varrho^{-3}Q(\varrho\cdot)

with
Q
the following second-order differential operator:

Q=Q^{\tau\tau}(r,\vartheta,\varphi_{*})\partial_{\tau}^{2}+Q^{\tau x}(r,\vartheta,\varphi_{*})\partial_{\tau}\partial_{x}+Q^{\tau\vartheta}(r,\vartheta,\varphi_{*})\partial_{\tau}\partial_{\vartheta}+Q^{\tau\varphi}(r,\vartheta,\varphi_{*})\partial_{\tau}\partial_{\varphi_{*}}+Q^{\tau}(r,\vartheta,\varphi_{*})\partial_{\tau}+\tilde{Q},

where
Q^{\tau},Q^{\tau\tau},Q^{\tau x},Q^{\tau\vartheta},Q^{\tau\varphi_{*}}
are bounded and
\tilde{Q}
is a differential operator of order
\leq 2
acting functions on
\Sigma
, such that
\tilde{Q}_{s}=\tilde{Q}:H_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}^{N_{+}}\to H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}
is bounded for all
|\sigma|<\sigma_{0}
, with
\sigma_{0}>0
or
\sigma_{0}=\infty
. Let
B
be the operator satisfying

\mathcal{A}+\delta\cdot B=\mathcal{A}_{\delta\cdot Q}.

Let
s_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
be such that
L_{s_{0}}^{-1}:H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}\to H_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}^{N_{+}+1}
is well-defined. Then the spectrum of
\mathcal{A}:{\rm dom}(\mathcal{A})\supset\mathbf{H}^{1}\to\mathbf{H}^{1}
is stable in
\{\re s>-(\frac{1}{2}+N_{+})\kappa_{+}\}\cap B_{\sigma_{0}^{2}}\cap\Omega=B_{\sigma_{0}^{2}}\cap\bigcup_{\sigma\in\mathbb{R}}\Omega_{\sigma,N_{+}}\subset\mathbb{C}
with respect to the families of subspaces
\{\mathbf{H}_{\sigma}^{N_{+}},D_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}(L_{s_{0}})\oplus H_{\sigma,R_{\infty},1}^{N_{+}+1})\}_{\sigma\in(-\sigma_{0},\sigma_{0})}
in the direction
B
.

In particular, if
Q
vanishes on
\Sigma\cap\{r\geq R_{\infty}\}
and has smooth coefficients, then the above stability result applies in the full
\Omega
.

Proof.

By a slight modification of the proof of Proposition
9.1
, we obtain the following relation:

\mathcal{A}_{\delta\cdot Q}-s\mathbf{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathbf{1}\\
\mathbf{1}&P_{\delta\cdot Q}-s\mathbf{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2M^{2}h(0)-Mxb_{\tau\tau}-\delta\cdot Q^{\tau\tau}}(L_{s}+\delta\cdot\tilde{Q})&0\\
0&\mathbf{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{1}&0\\
-s\mathbf{1}&\mathbf{1}\end{pmatrix},

with
P_{\delta\cdot Q}=\frac{2M^{2}h(0)-Mxb_{\tau\tau}}{2M^{2}h(0)-Mxb_{\tau\tau}-\delta\cdot Q^{\tau\tau}}P+\frac{\delta}{2M^{2}h(0)-Mxb_{\tau\tau}-\delta\cdot Q^{\tau\tau}}\left(Q^{\tau x}\partial_{x}+Q^{\tau\vartheta}\partial_{\vartheta}+Q^{\tau\varphi_{*}}\partial_{\varphi_{*}}+Q^{\tau}\mathbf{1}\right)
.

Note finally, that for all
\delta>0

P_{\delta\cdot Q}f\in H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}
if
f\in H_{\sigma,R_{\infty},1}^{N_{+}+1}
, so we can restrict the domain of
\mathcal{A}_{\delta\cdot Q}
to
D_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}(L_{s_{0}})\oplus H_{\sigma,R_{\infty},1}^{N_{+}+1}
.

By the above expression for
\mathcal{A}_{\delta\cdot Q}-s\mathbf{1}
and Corollary
9.2
, invertibility of
\mathcal{A}_{\delta\cdot Q}-s\mathbf{1}
is equivalent to invertibility of
L_{s}+\delta\cdot\tilde{Q}:H_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}^{N_{+}}\to H_{\sigma,R_{\infty},2}^{N_{+}+1}
, so we can apply Proposition
17.1
below to obtain the desired stability statement.
∎

The following theorem is a precise version of Theorem
4.2
(ii) (which leads to Theorem
1.2
(ii)).

Theorem 10.4
.

Let
\{\Lambda_{n}\}
be a sequence of cosmological constants satisfying
M^{2}\Lambda_{n}\ll 1
and
\Lambda_{n}\downarrow 0
. Suppose that there exists an
s_{*}\in\Omega
with
\ker L_{s_{*}}^{-1}\neq\{0\}
and consider
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
and
N_{+}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
s_{*}\in\Omega_{\sigma,N_{+}}
.

Then there exists a sequence
s_{n}\in\Omega_{\sigma,N_{+}}
, such that
\ker L_{s_{n},\Lambda_{n}}^{-1}\neq\{0\}
and
s_{n}\to s_{*}
as
n\to\infty
. Furthermore, for all
\widehat{\psi}\in\ker L_{s_{*}}^{-1}
, there exists a sequence
\{\widehat{\psi}_{n}\}
in
H^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}
with
\widehat{\psi}_{n}\in\ker L_{s_{n},\Lambda_{n}}^{-1}
and

\|\widehat{\psi}-\widehat{\psi}_{n}\|_{H^{N_{+}}_{\sigma,R_{\infty}}}\to 0\quad(n\to\infty).

Conversely, let
\{s_{n}\}
be a convergent sequence of elements in
\ker L_{s,\Lambda_{n}}
and
\Lambda_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, such that the limit
s_{*}
satisfies:
s_{*}\in\{|{\rm arg}z|<\frac{2\pi}{3}\}
. Then
s_{*}\in\ker L_{s,0}
.

Proof.

In the context of the
\kappa
-regularized operator
L_{s,\kappa}
, this proposition is proved in Lemma
16.3
. This is then generalized to the true Kerr–de Sitter operator
L_{s,\Lambda}
in §
18
.
∎

10.3.
Application 2: a characterization of scattering resonances

In order to justify the use of the term “regularity quasinormal frequencies”, we need to make a connection with other definitions of “quasinormal frequencies” that have previously appeared in the literature.

It is common to define “quasinormal frequencies” as scattering resonances associated to the meromorphic continuation of the cut-off resolvent operator corresponding to a “standard” time function whose level sets are asymptotically flat hypersurfaces. In the case of Kerr, this is typically take to be the Boyer–Lindquist time function
t
. One first considers the operator

A(\omega)=e^{i\omega t}\square_{g_{M,a}}(e^{-i\omega t}\cdot(\cdot)),

which can be inverted on suitable spaces of functions on
\Sigma^{\prime}=\{t=0\}
with so-called outgoing boundary conditions and where
\omega\in\mathbb{C}
with suitably large
\im\omega>0
. The inverse, denoted
R(\omega)
, is also called the
resolvent operator
.

While
R(\omega)
fails to make sense in a region intersecting both
\{\im z>0\}
and
\{\im z<0\}\subset\mathbb{C}
, one can conjugate
R(\omega)
with a multiplication operator
\chi\mathbf{1}
, where
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Sigma^{\prime})
and try to meromorphically continue the
cut-off resolvent
:

\chi R(\omega)\chi

as an operator on suitable function spaces. The poles of the meromorphic continuation of
\chi R(\omega)\chi
are called
scattering resonances
.

The theorem below provides a more precise version of Theorem
1.1
(iii). This theorem demonstrates that scattering resonances form a
subset
of the set of regularity quasinormal frequences, thereby justifying the nomenclature.

Theorem 10.5
.

Let
N_{+}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. The cut-off resolvent operator:

\chi R(\omega)\chi:H^{N_{+}}_{\not{\nabla}}(\Sigma^{\prime})\to H^{N_{+}+1}_{\not{\nabla}}(\Sigma^{\prime})

is well-defined for any cut-off function
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Sigma^{\prime})
and for all
\omega\in\mathbb{C}
with
\im\omega>\omega_{0}>0
suitably large and
\omega\mapsto\chi R(\omega)\chi
can be meromorphically continued to
i\Omega\cap\{\im\omega>-\left(\frac{1}{2}+N_{+}\right)\kappa_{+}\}
. Let
i\mathscr{Q}_{\rm res}
denote the union over the set of poles of
\omega\mapsto\chi R(\omega)\chi
over
N_{+}
. Then

\mathscr{Q}_{\rm res}\subseteq\mathscr{Q}_{\rm reg}.

Furthermore, for
\frac{|a|}{M}
suitably small, or under the restriction to functions with a bounded azimuthal number
m
,

\chi R(\omega)\chi:L^{2}(\Sigma^{\prime})\to H^{2}(\Sigma^{\prime})

is well-defined and can be meromorphically continued to
i\Omega
, with poles in the set
\mathscr{Q}_{\rm res}
.

Proof.

Consider the extension of
\Sigma^{\prime}
to the bifurcation sphere, denoted
\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}}
. By standard local-in-time energy estimates (see also the proof of Theorem
10.2
), it follows that the time evolution map
\mathcal{S}(\tau):H^{1}(\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}})\times L^{2}(\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}})\to H^{1}(\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}})\times L^{2}(\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}})
corresponding to initial data
(\phi,\partial_{t}\phi)
on
\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}}
is a bounded linear operator for each
\tau
, with a norm that can be bounded by
e^{s_{0}\tau}
, for suitably large
s_{0}>0
. By standard results of functional analysis, see for example
[
Eva98
]
[§7.4, Theorem 2] and
[
Hil48
]
[Theorem 11.6.1], the corresponding infinitesimal generator
\mathcal{A}:H^{1}(\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}})\times L^{2}(\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}})\supset{\rm dom}(\mathcal{A})\to H^{1}(\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}})\times L^{2}(\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}})
is closed and densely defined with
(\mathcal{A}-s\mathbf{1})^{-1}:H^{1}(\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}})\times L^{2}(\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}})\to{\rm dom}(\mathcal{A})
a well-defined bounded operator for
\re s>s_{0}
.

In this case, we can apply (
9.1
), with
\frac{L_{s}}{(2Mh(0)-Mxb_{\tau\tau})}
replaced with
\frac{r^{2}A(\omega)}{\mathbbm{h}(2(r^{2}+a^{2})-\Delta\mathbbm{h})-a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}
, where
\mathbbm{h}=\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}
with
\omega=i\cdot s
and
P
replaced with an appropriate first order operator to conclude via (
9.2
) that:

R(\omega):\frac{\Delta}{r^{2}}L^{2}(\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}})\to H^{1}(\overline{\Sigma^{\prime}})

is well-defined for suitably large
\im\omega>0
and we can consider
\chi R(\omega)\chi:L^{2}(\Sigma^{\prime})\to H^{1}(\Sigma^{\prime})
.

In Corollary
19.3
below, we prove the existence of the meromorphic continuation of
\chi R(\omega)\chi
, defined on appropriate subspaces, and its relation to
\mathscr{Q}_{\rm reg}
.
∎

Remark 10.3
.

The results of Theorem
10.5
in fact apply also when considering cut-off resolvents with respect to time functions whose level sets intersect
\mathcal{H}^{+}
to the future of the bifurcation sphere and are asymptotically flat. We denote the level set corresponding to “time zero” in this case by
\widetilde{\Sigma}
. Now, the cut-off functions
\chi
are allowed to be supported near
\mathcal{H}^{+}
but the support remains compact. In the domains and codomains of the maps in Theorem
10.5
,
\Sigma^{\prime}
is then replaced with
\widetilde{\Sigma}
.

11.
Guide to the rest of the paper

We outline the structure of the rest of the paper and relate each section to the steps sketched in §
4
, which concern the operator
L_{s}
. We first focus on the
\Lambda=0
setting.

Theorem
4.1
(i):

•

In §
12
, we derive red-shift estimates and elliptic estimates (featuring a finite number of derivatives) and couple them to obtain a single estimate in the bounded region
\varrho\leq\eta^{-1}R_{\infty}
.

•

In §
13
, we derive Gevrey-type estimates (featuring an infinite number of derivatives) in the region
\varrho\geq 2R_{\infty}
: estimates with higher-order radial derivatives are derived in §
13.1
and estimates with lower-order radial derivatives are derived in §
13.2
.

•

The Gevrey estimates are coupled with the red-shift and elliptic estimates in §
14
to obtain a single, global estimate.

•

In §
15
, we establish the invertibility of
\mathfrak{L}_{s,\lambda,\kappa}
with
\kappa>0
and construct
\mathfrak{L}_{s,\lambda}^{-1}
as a
\kappa\downarrow 0
limit.

•

In §
16
, we establish the meromorphicity and Fredholm properties of
L_{s}^{-1}
.

Theorem
4.1
(ii) and Theorem
4.2
(ii):

•

In §
16
, we also establish the independence of the regularity quasinormal spectrum with respect to a change in Hilbert spaces, corresponding to different
N_{+}
,
R_{\infty}
and
\sigma
. We also establish Theorem
4.2
(ii) for the
\kappa
-regularized operator.

We subsequently prove Theorem
4.2
(i):

•

In §
17
, we establish stability of the quasinormal spectrum under suitably small perturbations at the level of
L_{s}
.

Since the analysis in the
\Lambda>0
case actually follows, with minor modifications, from the analysis that is already required for the
\Lambda=0
case, we only take
\Lambda>0
in §
18
, where we focus primarily on the differences with the
\Lambda=0
case. Theorem
4.1
(i) for
\Lambda>0
and Theorem
4.2
(ii) for the true Kerr–de Sitter operator follow from §
18
.

Finally, we establish the relation between regularity quasinormal frequencies and scattering resonances:

•

Theorem
4.1
(iii): In §
19
, we construct the meromorphic continuation of the cut-off resolvent operator and relate scattering resonances to regularity quasinormal frequencies.

12.
Red-shift and elliptic estimates

We will consider different types of estimates in the following two regions:

(A)

r_{+}\leq r\leq\frac{1}{2}R_{\infty}
,

(B)

\frac{1}{4}R_{\infty}\leq\varrho\leq\eta^{-1}R_{\infty}
,

with
\mathbb{R}\ni\eta<2
and where
R_{\infty}M^{-1}
will be chosen suitably large. We will later also need to restrict to suitably small
\eta
.

The estimates in (A) are the spacelike analogues of the spacetime
red-shift estimates
of Dafermos–Rodnianski
[
DR09
]
and they establish, in particular, control of the non-degenerate
H^{1}
norm of
\hat{\psi}\in C^{\infty}(\widehat{\Sigma})
in terms of the
L^{2}
norm of
\mathfrak{L}_{s,\lambda}\hat{\psi}
. These estimates rely crucially on the positivity of the surface gravity
\kappa_{+}
associated to the event horizon at
r=r_{+}
. In contrast with the spacetime red-shift estimates, which apply only to regions where
r
is sufficiently close to
r_{+}
, they hold in regions of arbitrarily large
r
.

Note that for
M^{-1}R_{\infty}
sufficiently large, the region (A) covers the ergoregion of Kerr, where the vector field
T
is not timelike, and therefore “standard” (degenerate) elliptic estimates do not hold.

The estimates in (B) are more similar to standard elliptic estimates, in the sense that they establish control of a (weighted)
H^{2}
norm of
\hat{\psi}\in C^{\infty}(\widehat{\Sigma})
in terms of a (weighted)
L^{2}
norm of
\mathfrak{L}_{s,\lambda}\hat{\psi}
and rely on the fact that the spacetime metric is sufficiently close to the Minkowski metric in appropriate coordinates, by the asymptotic flatness property of the Kerr metric. The main difference with standard elliptic estimates, is the addition of a degenerate weight function in the norms, which will play an important role in the coupling with the Gevrey estimates of §
13
.

12.1.
Red-shift estimate in (A)

We first consider the region with:

r_{+}\leq\varrho\leq\frac{1}{2}R_{\infty}.

Recall from (
7.10
) that in this region
\varrho=r
,
\vartheta=\theta
and we can write:

r^{-2}\rho^{2}\mathfrak{L}_{s,\lambda,\kappa}=r\Delta\partial_{r}^{2}(r^{-1}\cdot)+r\frac{d\Delta}{dr}\partial_{r}(r^{-1}\cdot)+2ar\partial_{r}\partial_{\varphi_{*}}(r^{-1}\cdot)+\not{\Delta}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}(\cdot)+sr(4Mr-2(1-\mathbbm{h})\Delta)\partial_{r}(r^{-1}\cdot)-\lambda\pi_{\leq l}.

Proposition 12.1
.

Let
\re s>-\frac{\kappa_{+}}{2}
and
\delta=1+2\kappa_{+}^{-1}\min\{\re s,0\}
. Then there exists a constant
C_{s}=C_{s}(\kappa_{+},s)>0
and a numerical constant
C>0
such that

(12.1)

\int_{r_{+}}^{\frac{1}{3}R_{\infty}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\frac{1}{2}(r-M)(1+2\kappa_{+}^{-1}\min\{\re s,0\})r^{\delta}|\partial_{r}(r^{-1}\widehat{\psi})|^{2}\\
+\frac{1}{2}(1+2\kappa_{+}^{-1}\min\{\re s,0\})r^{-3+\delta}\left[|\not{\nabla}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\widehat{\psi}|^{2}+\lambda|\pi_{\leq l}\widehat{\psi}|^{2}\right]\,d\upsigma dr\\
